33	\title{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters for Time Series Foundation Models}
34

49	\begin{document}
50	
51	\maketitle
52	
53	\begin{abstract}
54	Adapting a pretrained time series foundation model (TSFM) to a downstream task typically involves attaching a single adapter head to the frozen backbone. A natural extension is a \emph{mixture of expert adapters} with a learned router, so that different input windows can be served by different adapter topologies. We show that this natural extension \textbf{catastrophically fails} on TSFMs that use instance normalization (RevIN): routing entropy collapses to $0.000{\pm}0.000$, because RevIN strips the per-window mean and scale that the router needs for discriminative gating. We call this failure \textbf{normalization-induced routing collapse} and isolate it through seven converging causal controls---most strikingly, opposite entropy trajectories under identical unfreezing on identical data (Timer-XL rises $1.49{\to}1.60$ while MOMENT collapses $0.60{\to}0.00$)---while all eleven standard MoE-rescue mechanisms fail ($2.7{\times}$ worse; 720 runs). A mutual-information decomposition (Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}) formalizes this: normalization destroys routing MI by an amount equal to the information in the stripped statistics, and the resulting signal-ratio predictor identifies which datasets benefit ($\rho{=}{-}0.88$, $p{<}0.002$, $n{=}9$), including where it does \emph{not} help (Traffic, $R{=}0.14$). Because the diagnosis pinpoints the cause, the fix is unambiguous: \textbf{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)} routes on the raw, pre-normalization input while the backbone stays frozen. RR-MoA achieves \textbf{54/54 wins} ($p{<}0.001$) on the primary backbone, generalizes across four additional backbones (two negative controls confirm normalization-specificity), and produces a \textbf{Frozen Paradox} where frozen adapters beat full fine-tuning by $12$--$79\%$. A dual-stream extension, \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}}, achieves \textbf{parity-or-better with DLinear on $108/123$ cells} across six backbones ($65$ significant wins vs $11$ losses).
55	\end{abstract}
56	
57	\section{Introduction}
58	\label{sec:intro}
59	
60	Time series foundation models (TSFMs)---MOMENT~\citep{goswami2024moment}, TimesFM~\citep{das2024timesfm}, Chronos~\citep{ansari2024chronos}, Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl}, Moirai~\citep{woo2024moirai}, and others~\citep{lee2024units, rasul2024lagllama, shi2024timemoe, ekambaram2024ttm}---provide powerful pretrained representations for temporal tasks. Once a TSFM is pretrained, a practical question determines whether it is useful in production: \textit{how should the adapter that connects it to a downstream task be designed?}
61	
62	\textbf{The problem with one-size-fits-all adapters.} Today, every public TSFM benchmark adapts the backbone with a single static head: pool hidden states, project to the forecast horizon~\citep{goswami2024moment, das2024timesfm, nie2023patchtst, hu2022lora}. But real time series are non-stationary---ETTh1 mixes quiet baseline segments with sharp seasonal excursions---and a single adapter topology cannot be optimal for every input window. A natural remedy is a \emph{mixture of expert adapters}: a pool of topologically distinct heads with a learned router that assigns each window to the most suitable expert.
63	
64	\textbf{Why keep the backbone frozen?} Frozen-backbone adaptation enables deployment regimes that per-dataset models like DLinear~\citep{zeng2023dlinear} cannot serve: multi-tenant cloud serving with hot-swappable adapters, on-device deployment with over-the-air adapter updates, and cross-task reuse (Appendices~\ref{app:deployment},~\ref{app:benchmark}). As TSFMs scale toward billion-parameter regimes~\citep{shi2024timemoe}, frozen-backbone PEFT becomes the primary per-dataset adaptation path.
65	
66	\textbf{The collapse problem.} This natural mixture-of-experts approach \emph{catastrophically fails} on TSFMs that use instance normalization. When the standard MoE recipe~\citep{wang2022adamix, fedus2022switch}---routing on hidden states with load balancing---is applied to MOMENT with RevIN, routing entropy drops to $0.000\pm0.000$ in 7/12 unfrozen configurations (Table~\ref{tab:adamix}). The root cause is \textbf{normalization-induced co-adaptation}: the dominant expert receives larger gradients, which reshape the backbone to serve it even more, creating a feedback loop that collapses within ${\sim}50$ training steps (Figure~\ref{fig:trajectory}). Even with a frozen backbone, hidden-state routing still underperforms because RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin} strips the per-window mean and variance that the router needs to distinguish different input regimes. This is normalization-specific: Moirai~\citep{woo2024moirai}, which uses LayerNorm instead of RevIN, does \emph{not} collapse (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}).
67	
68	\textbf{Our fix.} Because the diagnosis pinpoints normalization as the cause, the fix is unambiguous: \textbf{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)} routes on the raw, pre-normalization input $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$ while keeping the backbone strictly frozen. Only the router and five canonical expert heads are trainable. Eleven standard MoE-rescue mechanisms (load balancing, entropy regularization, z-loss, ReLU routing, expert-choice) all fail to recover performance ($2.7{\times}$ worse than RR-MoA across 720 runs; Table~\ref{tab:rescue}), confirming that the problem is in the router's \emph{input}, not its \emph{optimization}.
69	
70	Our contributions are threefold:
71	
72	\textbf{(1)~Diagnosis: why MoE routing collapses on normalized TSFMs.} We prove a mutual-information decomposition (Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}) showing that instance normalization destroys routing MI by exactly the information in the stripped location-scale statistics. This yields a quantitative predictor---the \emph{routing signal ratio} $R(D)$---validated at $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ ($p{<}0.002$) across nine datasets, including correctly predicting where raw routing does \emph{not} help (Traffic, $R{=}0.14$; Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}). Seven converging causal controls isolate this mechanism, the most striking being opposite entropy trajectories under identical unfreezing: MOMENT collapses ($0.60{\to}0.00$) while Timer-XL (no RevIN) \emph{rises} ($1.49{\to}1.60$)---same code, same data, only the normalization differs.
73	
74	\textbf{(2)~Fix: Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA).} By routing on raw pre-normalization input, RR-MoA achieves \textbf{54/54 wins} ($p{<}0.001$, Wilcoxon; Table~\ref{tab:baselines}) over LoRA, TRACE, ensembles, and full fine-tuning. We observe a \textbf{Frozen Paradox}: frozen RR-MoA beats full fine-tuning by $12$--$79\%$, because unfreezing triggers the co-adaptation loop that collapse depends on. RR-MoA generalizes across four backbones with instance normalization (MOMENT-small/large, Moirai, Moirai-MoE), while two negative controls without it (Chronos, Timer-XL) confirm normalization-specificity.
75	
76	\textbf{(3)~Closing the DLinear gap.} A dual-stream extension, Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}, adds a raw-input prediction branch to close the gap between frozen-backbone adapters and from-scratch baselines: it achieves parity-or-better with DLinear on $108/123$ cells across six backbones, \textbf{beating} DLinear on Weather ($0.198$ vs $0.208$, $p{=}0.03$; \S\ref{sec:main_results}).
77	
78	\section{Related Work}
79	
80	\textbf{TSFM Adaptation.} The standard approach to adapting a frozen TSFM is to attach a lightweight module---LoRA~\citep{hu2022lora}, bottleneck adapters~\citep{houlsby2019adapters}, or a linear probe~\citep{goswami2024moment}---and tune it on downstream data. Recent work has expanded the design space: MSFT~\citep{qiao2025msft} proposes multi-scale finetuning, TRACE~\citep{li2025trace} introduces importance-based LoRA selection with reconstructed heads, and AdaPTS~\citep{benechehab2025adapts} adapts input representations rather than the backbone itself. Beyond LoRA~\citep{gupta2024beyondlora} surveys PEFT alternatives for Chronos; Prune-then-Finetune~\citep{zhao2025prune} applies structured pruning; in-context fine-tuning~\citep{faw2025icf} adapts at inference time. TEMPLATE~\citep{zhang2025template} and GIFT-Eval~\citep{woo2024gifteval} address model selection and benchmarking. All of these search over finetuning \textit{strategies}---learning rates, rank, which layers to tune---rather than the adapter \textit{architecture} itself. Our work addresses this gap: we show that the choice of adapter topology should be \emph{per-sample}, not per-dataset.
81	
82	\textbf{MoE in Foundation Models.} Mixture-of-experts (MoE) routing~\citep{fedus2022switch} has been applied \emph{within} TSFM backbones to scale model capacity: Moirai-MoE~\citep{liu2025moiraimoe} routes frequency bands across internal experts, and Time-MoE~\citep{shi2024timemoe} uses sparse activation to reach billions of parameters. These designs place experts inside the backbone architecture. RR-MoA instead applies MoE at the \emph{adapter level} on a frozen backbone---an orthogonal design axis. We validate that the two axes stack: RR-MoA on top of Moirai-MoE still yields $-37\%$ MSE (\S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}, Table~\ref{tab:moirai_moe}).
83	
84	\textbf{MoE routing collapse and rescue.} Routing collapse---where a few experts capture all traffic---is a well-known failure mode in MoE training. Existing solutions attack it from the \emph{optimization side}: load-balancing losses~\citep{fedus2022switch}, z-loss~\citep{zoph2022stmoe}, Expert-Choice routing~\citep{zhou2022expertchoice}, and ReLU gating~\citep{zhang2025remoe}. AdaMix~\citep{wang2022adamix} brings MoE to adapter fine-tuning in NLP but still routes on hidden states. All of these enforce diversity through training objectives without asking whether the router's \emph{input} contains discriminative information in the first place. We sweep eleven such mechanisms on MOMENT$+$RevIN and show \textbf{none recover MSE to within $2.7{\times}$ of RR-MoA} (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}). Our contribution is complementary: we show that when normalization structurally removes routing information from hidden states, no optimization trick can compensate. Concurrently, recent work in NLP and vision has moved toward eliminating external routers entirely: AoE~\citep{lv2025aoe} lets experts self-select via activation norms, ERMoE~\citep{cheng2025ermoe} routes via cosine similarity to learned eigenbases (achieving 0\% collapse), and Routing-Free MoE~\citep{liu2026routingfree} encapsulates gating within each expert. We validate this self-routing principle in our setting (\S\ref{sec:main_results}): self-gated experts on raw input match RR-MoA, while the same architecture on hidden states collapses---confirming the bottleneck is normalization, not router design.
85	
86	\textbf{Normalization in Time Series.} RevIN~\citep{kim2021revin} mitigates distribution shift; DishTS~\citep{fan2023dishts} and Non-stationary Transformers~\citep{liu2022nonstationary} partially recover stripped statistics---both recognizing that instance normalization discards predictive information. We identify a previously unknown consequence: RevIN destroys the per-window statistics that MoE routers require for discriminative gating. RR-MoA operationalizes the ``statistic re-injection'' insight specifically for routing.
87	
88	\textbf{NAS and LLM-Guided Search.} NAS~\citep{zoph2017nas} has evolved through ENAS~\citep{pham2018enas}, DARTS~\citep{liu2019darts}, and regularized evolution~\citep{real2019regularized}. Shears~\citep{munoz2024shears} and RZ-NAS~\citep{ji2025rznas} search adapter hyperparameters for LLMs; NAS-LoRA~\citep{chen2025naslora} targets vision adapters. FunSearch~\citep{romera2024funsearch}, EvoPrompting~\citep{chen2023evoprompting}, PartEvo~\citep{hu2025partevo}, and ReEvo~\citep{ye2024reevo} demonstrate LLM-guided program synthesis. We use this paradigm for expert pool construction (Appendix~\ref{app:aas_details}).
89	
90	\section{Method}
91	\label{sec:method}
92

7	**Original finding (2026-04-09):** The 39-169% MSE gap between frozen MOMENT + RR-MoA and DLinear was diagnosed as fundamentally unclosable from backbone features alone.

16	
17	**Residual-IA+ architecture:** Raw NLinear branch (primary predictor) + backbone additive residual with per-sample instance-adaptive gate. Four fixes from 1,025-run diagnostic sweep: (i) residual formulation, (ii) gate-init b=-2, (iii) 5-epoch warmup, (iv) shared NLinear matching DLinear's 49K param budget.

449	
450	\textbf{Why RevIN destroys routing: formal analysis.} The experimental evidence above converges on a clear mechanism, but relies on empirical observations. We now formalize it through an information-theoretic lens. Each input window $\mathbf{x}_i$ decomposes into three components: mean $M_i = \mu(\mathbf{x}_i)$, scale $\Sigma_i = \sigma(\mathbf{x}_i)$, and shape $\mathbf{S}_i = (\mathbf{x}_i - M_i)/\Sigma_i$. RevIN maps $\mathbf{x}_i \mapsto \mathbf{S}_i$, discarding $(M_i, \Sigma_i)$. The following proposition quantifies how much routing information is lost.
451	
452	\begin{proposition}[Routing Information Loss Under RevIN]
453	\label{thm:mi_decomp}
454	Let $X$ be a random input window, $E \in \{1,\ldots,K\}$ the expert assignment produced by any deterministic router, and $S = (X - M)/\Sigma$ the RevIN-normalized shape.
455	\begin{enumerate}
456	    \item[\textup{(i)}] \textbf{Exact decomposition.} $I(X; E) - I(S; E) = I(M, \Sigma; E \mid S) \geq 0$.
457	    \item[\textup{(ii)}] \textbf{Unconditional quantitative bound.} Without any independence assumption,
458	    \[
459	        I(X; E) - I(S; E) \;\geq\; I(M, \Sigma;\, E) \;-\; I(M, \Sigma;\, S),
460	    \]
461	    where the residual $\varepsilon := I(M, \Sigma;\, S) \geq 0$ measures the ``leakage'' of stripped statistics through the shape.  Equality (tightness) holds iff $(M,\Sigma) \perp\!\!\!\perp S$ given $E$.
462	    \item[\textup{(iii)}] \textbf{Full-information corner.} If the router depends \emph{entirely} on the stripped statistics ($E \perp\!\!\!\perp S \mid (M,\Sigma)$), then $I(X; E) - I(S; E) = H(E)$: RevIN destroys the \emph{entire} routing distribution in nats.
463	    \item[\textup{(iv)}] \textbf{Empirical leakage and signal ratio.} The leakage $\varepsilon$ varies by dataset ($0.09$--$1.26$ nats via CCA; cross-validated within ${\pm}0.2$ nats by the distribution-free KSG estimator~\citep{kraskov2004ksg}). The signal ratio $R(\mathcal{D}) = [\mathrm{Var}(M) + \mathrm{Var}(\Sigma)] / \overline{\mathrm{Var}(S)}$ serves as a monotone proxy for the \emph{potential} routing information in the stripped statistics---validated at $\rho{=}{-}0.88$ across nine datasets. \textbf{Tightness:} direct measurement of $H(E \mid S)$ via held-out classifier confirms normalization retains only $16$--$41\%$ of routing entropy; the bound~(ii) gap averages $0.075$ nats ($4.7\%$ of $\log K$; Appendix~\ref{app:mi_tightness}).\footnote{$H(E \mid S)$ does not correlate with $\Delta\%$ ($\rho{=}{-}0.12$); $R(\mathcal{D})$ predicts improvement because it measures routing \emph{potential}, not the MI loss itself.}
464	\end{enumerate}
465	\end{proposition}
466	
467	Full proof in Appendix~\ref{app:proofs}. \textit{Remark.} KSG cross-check (${\pm}0.26$ nats) confirms leakage estimates hold beyond Gaussianity. \textbf{Independent validation:} training $\alpha \in [0,1]$ as an \texttt{nn.Parameter}, gradient descent converges to $\alpha < 0.5$ on all six datasets ($30/30$ runs; Appendix~\ref{app:learnable_alpha}).
468	
469	\textbf{Quantitative prediction.} The \emph{routing signal ratio} $R(\mathcal{D}) = [\text{Var}(M) + \text{Var}(\Sigma)] / \overline{\text{Var}(S)}$ serves as a monotone proxy for the MI bound. Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio} validates this across nine LTSF benchmarks: $\rho = -0.88$ ($p < 0.002$; bootstrap CI: $[-1.00, -0.51]$; $n{=}9$). Traffic ($R{=}0.14$) is correctly predicted as a boundary case ($\Delta{=}{+}2.9\%$). Solar ($R{=}0.06$) is the one outlier: the Conv1d router exploits temporal shape patterns beyond location-scale, consistent with the SSR ablation ($+43\%$ degradation). Additional ablations (SSR, RDGF, per-quartile analysis) in Appendix~\ref{app:routing_ablations}.

543	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Cross-backbone generalization.}\label{sec:cross_backbone} RR-MoA is not specific to MOMENT. On MOMENT-small and MOMENT-large (both using RevIN), it wins \textbf{all 21} configurations by $27$--$81\%$ (Table~\ref{tab:cross_backbone}). On Moirai (LayerNorm only, no RevIN), it wins \textbf{18/18} by $3$--$31\%$. On Moirai-MoE~\citep{liu2025moiraimoe}, which already contains sparse internal experts, RR-MoA still improves \textbf{all 6 datasets} by $37$--$90\%$ (Table~\ref{tab:moirai_moe})---confirming that adapter-level routing and backbone-internal routing are complementary. Two \textbf{negative controls} validate the diagnosis: Chronos and Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl}, which lack instance normalization, show no routing collapse (entropy $1.48$--$1.61$) and RR-MoA provides no advantage (\textbf{4/18} wins, within chance)---exactly as Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} predicts (Appendix~\ref{app:cross_backbone}).}
544	
545	{\looseness=-1 \textbf{Sparse routing, multi-horizon, and task generalization.}\label{sec:topk}
546	Top-2 sparse routing adds only $4.3$\,ms latency ($+10\%$) while retaining $63\%$ of the dense MSE gain. Scaling the expert pool from $K{=}3$ to $K{=}20$ changes MSE by less than $5\%$; notably, AdaMix at $K{=}15$ or $20$ maintains high entropy ($2.4$--$2.7$) yet its MSE remains $2$--$6{\times}$ worse than RR-MoA, confirming that the routing failure is informational rather than capacity-limited. RR-MoA's advantage extends beyond the primary horizon: it wins \textbf{24/24} configurations across $H{\in}\{96, 192, 336, 720\}$ and generalizes to imputation ($7/8$ datasets, $-44\%$ to $-77\%$) and classification ($86.5\%$ on BasicMotions; Appendix~\ref{app:topk},~\ref{app:imputation}).}
547	
548	\section{Conclusion}

> AGENT

Here's my honest assessment:

---

## Acceptance Probability: **55-65%**

This is slightly more conservative than the memory's 58-68% estimate. Here's my reasoning:

### Strengths (what works for you)

1. **Crisp, well-scoped diagnosis.** The normalization-induced routing collapse phenomenon is real, well-isolated (7 causal controls, Timer-XL opposite-trajectory proof), and the fix follows logically from the diagnosis. This is the kind of clean story NeurIPS reviewers appreciate.

2. **Overwhelming empirical evidence.** 4,259+ runs, 107 verified numeric claims, 54/54 wins on the primary backbone, ρ=-0.88 predictive validation across 9 datasets. The rescue sweep (720 runs, all 11 mechanisms fail) is particularly compelling — it preempts "why not just use load balancing?"

3. **Negative controls done right.** Chronos and Timer-XL showing no collapse (as predicted) is exactly the kind of falsifiable claim that builds credibility.

4. **Self-verification infrastructure.** 107 checks in verify.py is unusual rigor for a NeurIPS submission.

5. **Writing quality.** The abstract is tight, the claims are hedged appropriately ("54/54" not "always"), boundary cases acknowledged (Traffic, Solar).

### Weaknesses (what reviewers will attack)

1. **"Obvious fix" vulnerability (partially mitigated but still alive).** A skeptical reviewer can still summarize the paper as: "RevIN removes mean/scale → route on raw input instead." The Timer-XL causal proof and rescue sweep help, but the core intervention is a one-line change (swap router input). NeurIPS values novelty, and this may score 5-6 on novelty even with perfect execution.

2. **DLinear gap (partially closed, not gone).** Residual-IA+ gets 108/123, but the gap still exists on the primary backbone (MOMENT-small). A reviewer who believes "if DLinear beats your TSFM+adapter, why bother?" will not be fully satisfied by the "calibration anchor" framing. The honest answer — RevIN structurally limits what frozen adapters can do — is correct but unflattering.

3. **Proposition 2 is chain-rule accounting, not a theorem.** The MI decomposition is correct but essentially restates definitions. The empirical content (Part iv, R(D) correlation) is the real contribution, but a theory-focused reviewer may find the proposition insufficiently novel for a "theoretical contribution" claim. The tightness work (0.075 nats gap) helps but doesn't fully resolve this.

4. **Single primary backbone.** MOMENT-small is the workhorse. The cross-backbone results are strong but thinner (n=3-5 seeds vs n=5-10 on MOMENT). If the cross-backbone expansion on RACE finishes and pushes 62/72 → 80+/120, this weakness shrinks.

5. **Scope of impact.** The paper solves a problem specific to MoE routing on instance-normalized TSFMs. The audience who cares about this intersection is smaller than, say, a general MoE improvement. A reviewer may score high on soundness but lower on significance.

### Simulated Reviewer Scores

| Reviewer | Profile | Score | Reasoning |
|---|---|:---:|---|
| R1 | TSFM/PEFT specialist | **7** | Appreciates the diagnosis, respects the empirical depth, sees practical value |
| R2 | MoE/routing specialist | **6-7** | Likes the rescue sweep, but may find the fix unsurprising given the diagnosis |
| R3 | TS generalist | **6-7** | Solid execution, but questions DLinear gap and significance |
| R4 | Theory-focused | **5-6** | Prop 2 is chain rule, not novel math. MI tightness helps but doesn't fully compensate |

**Meta-reviewer**: With {7, 6.5, 6.5, 5.5} average ~6.4 → borderline accept. NeurIPS acceptance threshold is typically ~6.0, but variance is high.

### Key Risks

- **One hostile R4 at 4-5** drops the average to ~6.0, which is coin-flip territory
- **All three structural vulnerabilities are *mitigated* but not *eliminated*** — they're each worth ~0.5 points of deduction
- **NeurIPS 2026 competitiveness is unknowable** — acceptance rate matters

### What Would Move the Needle

The remaining ~2 weeks before deadline:
- **Cross-backbone expansion completing** (RACE results) → could push DLinear parity to 80+/120
- **Tighter framing of Proposition 2** as an "empirical information-theoretic principle" rather than a "theorem" would preempt R4's objection
- **One more backbone (e.g., PatchTST or TimesFM)** would strengthen generalization claims

**Bottom line:** This is a solid, well-executed empirical paper with a clean diagnosis story. It's above the NeurIPS bar on execution and below it on perceived novelty for some reviewers. 55-65% reflects a paper that gets in more often than not, but is not safe.

4	type: project

6	
7	## Dose-Response Curve (completed 2026-04-20, 180 runs, 0 failures)
8	
9	Alpha interpolation between raw (α=0) and RevIN (α=1) router input.
10	6 datasets × 5 alpha × 5 seeds + 30 shuffle runs.
11	
12	### Key findings:
13	
14	1. **Phase transition at α→1.0**: Mild degradation for α ∈ [0, 0.75] (mean +5.4% MSE), catastrophic at α=1.0 (mean +283.5%). Not linear — sharp cliff near full normalization.
15	
16	2. **MSE monotonicity: 4/6 strict** (ETTh1, ETTh2, Weather, Electricity). ETTm1 tiny dip at α=0.25 within noise. ETTm2 non-monotone dip at α=0.75 before spike.
17	
18	3. **Entropy stays high throughout** (1.43-1.57). Router doesn't collapse — it makes diversified but uninformative decisions under normalization. More subtle than pure entropy collapse.
19	
20	4. **Shuffle ≈ raw**: Shuffled routing performs within ±3% of raw on 4/6 datasets, ±16% on ETTm2. Routing depends on distributional statistics, not temporal patterns. Consistent with Prop 2.
21	
22	5. **MI gap = 0 everywhere**: I(M,Σ;E) < ε for all 9 datasets. Formal bound is loose. R(D) proxy works better (ρ=-0.88) than calibrated MI gap.
23	
24	### Paper impact:
25	- W1 ("obvious fix"): Phase transition shows the phenomenon is nuanced, not a binary switch
26	- W3 ("Prop 2 not tight"): Shuffle validates the M/Σ mechanism; R(D) remains the best predictor
27	- New figure: dose-response curve with phase transition annotation
28	
29	**Why:** Experimental evidence that directly addresses two structural weaknesses.
30	**How to apply:** Integrate as new figure in §4 or appendix. Frame phase transition as "small amount of raw signal preserves routing quality."
31

Perfect! Now I have a comprehensive understanding. Let me create a final summary report:

## Experiment Infrastructure Summary for NeurIPS 2026 Paper

Based on my thorough exploration of the codebase, here's the complete experiment infrastructure available for planning GPU experiments:

---

### 1. **`scripts/run_rr_moa.py`** — Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters

**Key CLI Arguments:**
- `--dataset` (default: ETTh1) — dataset selection
- `--horizon` (default: 96) — forecast horizon
- `--K` (default: 5) — number of expert adapters
- `--top-k` (default: None, i.e., dense) — sparse routing threshold
- `--router-input-mode` (choices: raw, revin, uniform, partial, shuffled) — router signal source
  - `raw` (default): routes on unnormalized input
  - `partial`: dose-response interpolation (requires `--alpha`)
  - `uniform`: forces 1/K weights (ensemble control)
  - `shuffled`: temporally permuted signal (mechanism ablation)
- `--alpha` (default: 0.0) — normalization dose for partial mode (0=raw, 1=full RevIN)
- `--unfreeze` (choices: frozen, last2, last4, all) — backbone unfreezing strategy
- `--expert-pool` (choices: canonical, macro, identical-mean, identical-conv1d, identical-attn)
  - `canonical`: 5 pooling heads (mean/last/max/attention/conv1d)
  - `macro`: AAS-discovered cross-domain motifs (integration with T3.A)
  - `identical-*`: diversity ablation (same architecture, different init)
- `--router-arch` (choices: conv, stats, ssr, multiscale, fft, linear) — router architecture
  - `conv` (default): Conv1d + pool + linear
  - `ssr`: 2-scalar sufficient statistics (Prop 2 proof)
  - `fft`: spectral-temporal dual-branch
  - `linear`: naive V1 ablation
- `--entropy-reg` (default: 0.0) — entropy regularization for diversity
- `--router-temp` (default: 1.0) — softmax temperature
- `--freeze-router` (bool) — V1 ablation: freeze router at random init
- `--expert-dropout` (default: 0.0) — dropout per expert
- `--saib-coef` (default: 0.0) — SAIB auxiliary loss coefficient
- `--rdgf` (bool) — Router-Detached Gradient Flow
- `--disable-revin` (bool) — ablation: disable RevIN in backbone
- `--epochs` (default: 15) — training epochs
- `--seed` (default: 42), `--device`, `--backbone` (default: AutonLab/MOMENT-1-small)

**Output Directory:** `results/rr_moa/`

**Output Format:** JSON with keys:
- `rr_moa`: {mse, mae, param_count, routing, routing_entropy, routing_max_weight, routing_per_sample_std, routing_cross_sample_var}
- `baselines`: {linear, attention, conv}
- `winner`, `delta_pct`, `scaler` (denormalization info)

**Approximate Scope:** 
- Core table: 1 dataset × 1 seed = 1 config (5 min/config)
- Dose-response sweep (alpha ∈ {0, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 1}): 6 datasets × 5 alphas × 5 seeds = 150 configs
- Router ablations (ssr, stats, fft, linear): 4 variants × 6 datasets × 5 seeds = 120 configs

---

### 2. **`scripts/run_adamix.py`** — Learned Adapter Mixture

**Key CLI Arguments:**
- `--K` (default: 5), `--hidden` (default: 64)
- `--unfreeze` (frozen, last2, last4, all) — unfreezing strategy
- **MoE Rescue Mechanisms (Section 3 rescue sub-table):**
  - `--router-type` (softmax, relu, expert-choice):
    - `softmax` (default): Switch Transformer gating
    - `relu`: ReMoE ReLU gating (Wang et al. ICLR 2025)
    - `expert-choice`: Zhou et al. 2022 per-expert top-k
  - `--load-balance-coef` (default: 0.01) — Switch aux loss coefficient
  - `--load-balance-variant` (mean-prob, argmax) — f_i computation
  - `--entropy-reg-coef` (default: 0.0) — entropy regularization (sweep: {0, 0.01, 0.1, 1.0})
  - `--z-loss-coef` (default: 0.0) — ST-MoE z-loss (sweep: {0, 0.001, 0.01, 0.1})
  - `--relu-l1-coef` (default: 0.0) — ReMoE L1 sparsity (sweep: {0.001, 0.01, 0.1})
  - `--capacity-factor` (default: 2.0) — expert-choice capacity
- `--router-input` (hidden, raw) — Exp D ablation: route on raw vs hidden
- `--trajectory` — path to JSONL file for T2.A mechanistic trajectory
- `--disable-revin`, `--norm-type` (revin, batchnorm, groupnorm)
- `--run-baselines` (auto, yes, no) — skipped during rescue sweeps
- `--results-dir` (auto-switches to `results/adamix_rescue/` if rescue flags set)

**Output Directory:** `results/adamix/` or `results/adamix_rescue/` (for rescue sweeps)

**Rescue Mechanisms Already Implemented:**
1. Softmax gating (legacy)
2. ReLU gating (ReMoE)
3. Expert-choice routing (per-expert top-k)
4. Load-balance variants (f_i = P_i vs f_i = argmax)
5. Entropy regularization (encourages routing diversity)
6. z-loss (stabilizes training)
7. L1 sparsity (ReMoE-specific)
8. Raw-input router (Exp D: proves diagnosis is architecture-agnostic)

**Approximate Scope:** 
- Rescue sweep: 6 datasets × 10+ rescue configs × 5 seeds = 300+ configs
- Trajectory logging: 1 config per dataset produces detailed per-step routing/gradient diagnostics

---

### 3. **`scripts/run_gap_closing.py`** — Raw-Input Informed Variants

**Key CLI Arguments:**
- `--variant` (required; choices below)
- `--top-k` (default: 2), `--K` (default: 5)
- `--raw-hidden` (default: 192) — hidden dim for raw branch
- `--raw-depth` (1, 2, 3) — raw branch depth
- `--raw-arch` (linear, nlinear) — NLinear trick for non-stationary
- `--raw-branch-shared` (bool) — share one raw branch across all experts (DLinear budget)
- `--gate-init` (default: -2.0) — initial bias for instance-adaptive gates
- `--warmup-epochs` (default: 0) — epochs to train raw branch only
- `--val-early-stop`, `--val-patience` — early stopping config
- `--cosine`, `--lr`, `--weight-decay`, `--grad-clip` — optimization
- `--noise-sigma` — robustness ablation

**Variants & Architecture Details:**

1. **dual-stream**: Each expert blends backbone + raw branches with learnable static alpha
   - Params: 5 experts × (backbone_head + raw_branch + alpha_param)
   
2. **film**: FiLM conditioning re-injects (μ, σ) from raw input into hidden states
   - Adds a modulation head: raw stats → (γ, β) for H' = H(1+γ) + β
   
3. **raw-expert**: 5 backbone experts + 1 raw-bypass expert (6 total)
   - Raw expert: Linear(input_len, output_dim), no backbone
   
4. **multi-res**: Concatenates projected raw + pooled hidden per expert
   - Each expert: [raw_proj || h_pool] → output
   
5. **residual-stream**: Raw branch is base, backbone branch predicts residual
   - output = raw_branch(x) + backbone_branch(H)
   
6. **ia-gating**: Instance-Adaptive gating (per-window gate, learnable)
   - gate from router_feat → sigmoid → scales backbone contribution
   
7. **residual-ia** (key variant for DLinear gap closure):
   - Raw branch: primary forecaster (DLinear-like by default)
   - Backbone: additive residual scaled by per-sample sigmoid gate
   - `--raw-depth` = 1: pure linear (DLinear exact); = 2: default; = 3: deep
   - Diverse raw_hidden: [raw_hidden-64, ..., raw_hidden+64] per expert
   - Optional shared raw branch: single Linear(input_len, output_dim) across all experts

**Output Directory:** `results/gap_closing/`

**Approximate Scope:**
- 7 variants × 6 datasets × 5 seeds = 210 configs
- Residual-IA ablations: K ∈ {3, 5, 7}, raw_hidden ∈ {128, 192, 256}, gate_init ∈ {-3, -2, -1, 0}

---

### 4. **`scripts/run_self_routed_moa.py`** — Self-Routed Mixture of Adapters

**YES — Self-routing experiment exists!**

**Key CLI Arguments:**
- `--K` (default: 5), `--routing-mode` (gated, eigenbasis, hybrid)
- **Routing Mechanisms:**
  - `gated` (default): per-expert sigmoid gate on raw input
    - Each expert has: MLP(input_len) → 1 scalar → sigmoid
    - `--gate-hidden` (default: 16) — MLP hidden dim (1 = pure linear)
    - `--gate-init-bias` (default: 0.0) — negative values = start inactive
  - `eigenbasis`: cosine similarity to learned orthogonal basis vectors
    - Shared projection: Linear(input_len, basis_dim)
    - Per-expert basis vectors: orthogonally initialized
    - softmax(cos_sim / temperature)
  - `hybrid`: sigmoid of cosine similarity (combines both)
    - sigmoid(cos_sim / temperature) → renormalize
- `--basis-dim` (default: 64) — basis vector dimension
- `--temperature` (default: 0.1) — softmax/sigmoid scaling
- `--routing-input` (raw, hidden) — control experiment: route on hidden vs raw
- `--unfreeze`, `--seed`, `--epochs`, `--backbone`

**Key Property:** No external router; each expert self-determines activation from raw input. Tests ERMoE/AoE/Routing-Free MoE hypothesis.

**Output Directory:** `results/self_routed_moa/`

**Approximate Scope:**
- 3 modes × 6 datasets × 5 seeds = 90 configs
- Basis/gate hyperparameter ablations: temperature ∈ {0.01, 0.1, 1.0}, basis_dim ∈ {16, 64, 128}

---

### 5. **`scripts/run_vision_moe_collapse_v2.py`** — Cross-Modal Routing Collapse Proof

**Key CLI Arguments:**
- `--seed` (default: 42), `--epochs` (default: 20), `--batch-size` (default: 128)
- `--device` (cuda/cpu), `--lr` (default: 1e-3), `--data-dir`, `--output-dir` (default: results/vision_moe_v2)

**Test 4 Conditions (Automaticially Run):**
- A) Unfrozen + InstanceNorm2d → expect collapse
- B) Frozen + InstanceNorm2d → expect no collapse
- C) Unfrozen + No norm → expect no collapse
- D) Unfrozen + BatchNorm2d → expect collapse

**Architecture:**
- Backbone: ResNet-18 on CIFAR-10, output (B, 512, H, W)
- 5 expert heads: mean-pool, max-pool, attention-pool, conv-pool, last-patch
- Router: pool normalized features → gate logits
- No load-balancing loss (natural specialization allowed)

**Output:** 
- Directory: `results/vision_moe_v2/`
- Files: `trajectory_{condition}_{seed}.jsonl` (per-step diagnostics)
- File: `collapse_summary_{seed}.json` (summary metrics)

**Approximate Scope:** All 4 conditions × 1 seed = 1 comprehensive run (~30-60 min on GPU)

- Compares with shuffle ablation

**Approximate Scope:** 6 datasets × 5 alphas × 5 seeds = 150 configs

## Backbone Support & Cross-Backbone Expansion Analysis

### 1. **Supported Backbones in `feasibility/model.py`**

The `load_backbone()` function has a clean dispatch architecture with **5 supported TSFM backbones**:

| Backbone | Function | HF Model ID (default) | Status |
|----------|----------|----------------------|--------|
| **MOMENT** | `load_moment()` | `AutonLab/MOMENT-1-small` | Primary (used in most scripts) |
| **Chronos** | `load_chronos()` | `amazon/chronos-t5-small` | Fully supported |
| **Moirai** | `load_moirai()` | `Salesforce/moirai-1.1-R-small` | Fully supported |
| **Moirai-MoE** | `load_moirai_moe()` | `Salesforce/moirai-moe-1.0-R-small` | Fully supported (native MoE backbone) |
| **Timer** | `load_timer()` | `thuml/timer-base-84m` | Fully supported |

**Dispatch logic** (`load_backbone()`, lines 122-142 in `feasibility/model.py`):
- Checks substring matches in order: MOMENT → Chronos → Timer → Moirai-MoE → Moirai
- **Critical**: Moirai-MoE MUST be checked BEFORE Moirai (dispatcher guards against substring collision)
- Each loader returns `.encoder` or `.model.layers` with consistent interface
- **No PatchTST or TimesFM support** — not imported or referenced anywhere

---

peft>=0.7.0

```

**What's missing from requirements.txt but used in code:**
- `chronos` (PyPI: `chronos-forecasting`)
- `uni2ts` (Moirai: PyPI: `uni2ts`)
- `transformers` (for Timer via `AutoModelForCausalLM`)

**Assumption**: These are installed separately on RACE VM (likely via `pip install chronos uni2ts transformers` before running experiments).

**Not on RACE**: PatchTST, iTransformer, TimesFM, Informer, Autoformer, FEDformer — none referenced.

### 4. **Cross-Backbone Results in `results/rr_moa/`**

```

_bb-moirai            → Salesforce/moirai-1.1-R-small
_bb-moirai-moe        → Salesforce/moirai-moe-1.0-R-small
_bb-moment-large      → AutonLab/MOMENT-1-large
_bb-timer-base-84m    → thuml/timer-base-84m
```

**Evidence from directory listing**: Results show systematic cross-backbone runs (e.g., `ETTh1_H96_K5_top2_frozen_42_bb-chronos.json` alongside `_bb-moirai.json`, `_bb-timer-base-84m.json`, etc.).

**Backbone suffix generation logic** (`run_rr_moa.py`, lines 697-708):
```python
if "large" in args.backbone.lower():
    suffixes.append("bb-moment-large")
elif "moe" in args.backbone.lower():
    suffixes.append("bb-moirai-moe")
elif "moirai" in args.backbone.lower():
    suffixes.append("bb-moirai")
elif "chronos" in args.backbone.lower():
    suffixes.append("bb-chronos")
elif args.backbone != "AutonLab/MOMENT-1-small":
    suffixes.append("bb-" + args.backbone.split("/")[-1].lower())
```

---

### 5. **Feature Extraction Bridge Across Backbones**

The `_extract_features_batch()` function in `/feasibility/finetune.py` (lines 102-138) **abstracts away backbone differences**:

```python
def _extract_features_batch(model, encoder_blocks, batch_x, input_mask, backbone_type="moment"):
    if backbone_type == "chronos":
        _forward_chronos(model, encoder_blocks, batch_x)
    elif backbone_type == "moirai":
        _forward_moirai(model, encoder_blocks, batch_x)
    elif backbone_type == "timer":
        _forward_timer(model, batch_x)
    else:
        # MOMENT fallback
        model(x_enc=batch_x, input_mask=input_mask)
```

Each backbone has a specialized forward handler:
- **MOMENT**: Passes `x_enc` + `input_mask` to pipeline
- **Chronos**: Patches 512-dim input, pads to d_model=384, forward through encoder
- **Moirai**: Patches + uses `model.in_proj` multi-scale projection
- **Timer**: Raw input (internal 96-token patching), causal decoder-only forward

**All return** `(B, seq_len, d_model)` hidden states, standardizing the interface.

---

### 6. **Additional TSFMs Referenced but NOT Integrated**

**Mentioned in docstrings/comments but NO loader functions**:
- **DLinear** — minimal baseline (lines 32-40 in `run_dlinear_baseline.py`), NOT a TSFM backbone, just `nn.Linear(input_len → horizon)`
- **TimesFM** — cited in literature but no `load_timesfm()` function
- **PatchTST** — mentioned in legacy script `run_standard_benchmark.py` docstring (line 2) but no implementation
- **iTransformer** — mentioned in docstring but no implementation
- **Informer, Autoformer, FEDformer** — zero references

## What Would Need New Code for Cross-Backbone Expansion

### **To add a new TSFM backbone (e.g., PatchTST)**:

1. **Add loader in `feasibility/model.py`**:
```python
def load_patchtst(device: str = "cpu", model_name: str = "...") -> nn.Module:
    from gluonts.torch.model.patch_tst import PatchTSTEstimator
    model = PatchTSTEstimator.from_pretrained(model_name)
    return model.to(device).eval()
```

2. **Update `load_backbone()` dispatcher** (add BEFORE fallback):
```python
elif "patchtst" in lower:
    return load_patchtst(device, model_name=backbone_name)
```

3. **Add feature extraction handler** in `feasibility/finetune.py`:
```python
def _forward_patchtst(model, encoder_blocks, batch_x):
    # PatchTST-specific patching logic
    x = batch_x.squeeze(1)
    # ... patch + forward
    model.encoder(...)

# Update _extract_features_batch:
elif backbone_type == "patchtst":
    _forward_patchtst(model, encoder_blocks, batch_x)
```

4. **Update `_detect_backbone_type()`** in `run_standard_evolution.py`:
```python
if "patchtst" in name: return "patchtst"
```

5. **Update result suffix logic** in `run_rr_moa.py` (lines 698-708).

### **What's already abstracted (no changes needed)**:

- Adapter training loops (works with any d_model + encoder_blocks)
- RR-MoA router (routes on raw input, backbone-agnostic)
- Data loading pipeline (standardized StandardScaler normalization)
- Evaluation protocol (test set MSE/MAE on denormalized predictions)

The infrastructure for multi-backbone evaluation is **mature and systematic**. Adding new TSFMs is straightforward (5-10 min per model) but requires manual dispatch code rather than automatic detection.

## Summary: Missing Experiments to Strengthen the NeurIPS Paper

Based on my thorough exploration of the codebase, here's my analysis of what experiments are missing and what infrastructure already exists:

### (A) KEY EXPERIMENTS MISSING THAT WOULD ADDRESS WEAKNESSES

#### 1. **"Obvious Fix" Criticism — Show Non-Triviality Through Pathological Cases**

**Current state:** The paper demonstrates raw routing works well, but the fix appears trivially straightforward.

**Recommended experiments:**
- **Implicit routing signal preservation under partial normalization** (already in infrastructure but underexploited): The dose-response ablation (Figure 4, `scripts/run_gap_closing.py` with `--raw-arch` variants) shows a phase transition at α=1.0, but only on MOMENT-small. Run this across **all 6 backbones** to show:
  - Where the mechanism breaks down (which backbones preserve routing info despite RevIN?)
  - How dataset characteristics affect the threshold (is Solar's α-threshold different because of temporal patterns vs. statistics?)
  - Provide theoretical binding: validate Proposition 2 bound tightness per backbone

- **Router learned to exploit leakage gracefully (negative evidence)**: Train routers with maximum regularization to prevent learning the (M,Σ) "leakage" channels—does performance collapse faster? This would show raw input isn't just one solution among many, but the structural fix.

#### 2. **DLinear Gap — Narrow Backbone Coverage**

**Current state:** Residual-IA+ beats DLinear on 108/123 cells across 6 backbones, but:
- MOMENT-small shows +2.2% gap (largest failure case)
- No systematic study of why some backbones (Timer-XL: 12/12) succeed perfectly while others (MOMENT: 21/24) fail

**Recommended experiments:**
- **Ablate raw branch architecture systematically** (infrastructure exists but scattered):
  - `--raw-arch nlinear` (shift-subtract-linear) for MOMENT specifically (already tried, see gate-closing results)
  - **But missing:** What if raw branch uses the SAME RevIN preprocessing internally? Does re-normalizing the raw branch help MOMENT close the gap?
  - **Test heterogeneous raw branches:** Let each expert's raw branch choose depth ∈ {1,2,3} during training (`--raw-depth` exists but only per-run, not per-expert)

- **Investigate MOMENT's peculiar co-adaptation with residual structure:**
  - Run residual-ia with gate_init sampled per-expert: does the backbone contribute more on some channels?
  - Does unfreezing last-4 blocks on residual-ia + MOMENT close the gap further? (currently only frozen is tested systematically)

- **Comparative study on why Timer-XL/Chronos succeed (12/12) while MOMENT struggles:**
  - Both avoid RevIN (LayerNorm-only), but Timer-XL is decoder-only, Chronos is T5-style
  - Run the **same residual-ia config** on T5-style backbones (Chronos) vs. decoder-style (Timer-XL) to isolate: is the gap architectural or dataset-specific?

#### 3. **Narrow Backbone Coverage — Missing Modern Baselines**

**Current state:** Main results use MOMENT-small (2B params) with lighter coverage on large/others. Paper mentions:
- MOMENT-large: exists but thinner results
- Moirai-MoE: only validated to show "stacking" (+37% improvement)
- **Missing:** No systematic evaluation of whether raw routing scales to 8B+ parameter models (Moirai-MoE, Timer-XL)

**Recommended experiments:**
- **Scale test:** Run residual-ia on MOMENT-large, Moirai-MoE across all 6 datasets at H=96 (currently only spot checks exist). This addresses scalability skepticism.

- **PatchTST integration:** The paper mentions PatchTST once in baselines (0.370 MSE, from-scratch baseline), but never tests if RR-MoA/residual-ia can compete as an adapter on top of frozen PatchTST. PatchTST is non-transformer (patch-embedding CNN-based), so routing behavior might differ fundamentally.

#### 4. **Proposition 2 ("Frozen Paradox") — Deeper Mechanism**

**Current state:** Prop 1 is rigorous (MI decomposition), but Prop 2 is described as "chain-rule accounting, not deep theory":
- Linear algebra statement about co-adaptation loop
- Confirmed empirically on linear models + observed on Transformers (Figure 5b, 50 steps)

**Recommended experiments:**
- **Quantify co-adaptation feedback strength by layer:**
  - Already infrastructure exists to unfreeze last-k blocks. Run full suite on last-1, last-2, last-4, last-8 to show entropy collapse *profile* (which layers contribute most to co-adaptation?)
  - Plot entropy trajectories (not just endpoint) for unfrozen=last-1 vs last-4 vs all to show feedback strength scales

- **Test co-adaptation robustness to initialization:**
  - Re-initialize router weights mid-training (epoch 8 of 15) on a "collapsed" run. Does entropy recover?
  - This isolates whether collapse is structural (inevitable) or path-dependent (router got stuck)

- **Cross-expert co-adaptation measurement:** Currently only aggregate entropy is tracked. Compute per-expert contribution drift: which experts co-adapt with backbone most?

#### 5. **Self-Routing Results Are Underexploited**

**Current state:** 
- `scripts/run_sr_moa.py` (spectral/FFT routing) exists but only 3 seeds on 6 datasets
- `scripts/run_self_routed_moa.py` (gated/eigenbasis self-routing) exists but no results directory or paper figures

**Recommended experiments:**
- Run self-routed modes ("gated", "eigenbasis", "hybrid") across all 6 datasets × 3 seeds and **compare vs RR-MoA**. The paper claims "self-gated experts on raw input match RR-MoA" but provides no quantitative evidence in results folder.

- **Theory test:** If normalization destroys routing, self-routing on *hidden states* should collapse identically to standard routing. Run `--routing-input hidden` on self-routed to confirm diagnosis.

---

### (B) INFRASTRUCTURE ALREADY AVAILABLE FOR REUSE

#### 1. **Gap-Closing Variants (Most Developed)**
- **Location:** `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/scripts/run_gap_closing.py`
- **Variants implemented:**
  - `dual-stream`: per-expert learnable alpha blend (99 runs in results)
  - `residual-ia`: raw branch primary + backbone residual with per-expert gate (1388 runs—most extensive)
  - `ia-gating`: instance-adaptive per-expert gates
  - `film`: FiLM modulation to re-inject stripped statistics
  - `raw-expert`: 6th expert that bypasses backbone entirely
  - `multi-res`: concatenated raw + pooled features
  - `residual-stream`: additive residual (no gating)

- **Reusable hyperparameters:** `--raw-hidden`, `--raw-depth` (1=linear, 2=MLP, 3=deep), `--gate-init` (control raw-dominance), `--raw-arch` (linear vs. nlinear), `--warmup-epochs` (freeze backbone for first N epochs), `--val-early-stop` with patience

- **Infrastructure for cross-backbone testing:** Backbone selection via `--backbone` with auto-detection in `_detect_backbone_type()`. Tested on: MOMENT-small/large, Moirai, Moirai-MoE, Chronos, Timer-XL.

#### 2. **Rescue Baseline Sweep (Already Complete)**
- **Location:** `main.tex` Table 3 + Appendix references
- **Coverage:** 11 MoE mechanisms × 6 datasets × 2 freeze levels × 5 seeds = 720 runs
- **Reusable:** Weights, loss configs for each mechanism are tuned. If proposing a new rescue mechanism, this provides the baseline.

#### 3. **Self-Routing Implementation**
- **Location:** `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/scripts/run_self_routed_moa.py`
- **Modes:** `gated` (sigmoid per expert), `eigenbasis` (cosine similarity to learned bases), `hybrid` (sigmoid of cosine)
- **Control experiments built-in:** `--routing-input` can switch to `hidden` to test if the mechanism survives on normalized hidden states (should collapse, validating diagnosis)

#### 4. **Analysis Pipeline (MI Estimation)**
- **Location:** `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/results/analysis/`
- **Artifacts:**
  - `exact_mi_loss.json`: MI measurements per dataset (estimated via held-out classifier)
  - `routing_signal_ratio.json`: R(D) values for 9 datasets (validates Proposition 1)
  - `synthetic_mi_tightness.json`: Bound tightness validation on synthetic data
- **Reusable:** MI estimator code (CCA, KSG) can be adapted for new statistics

#### 5. **Training Infrastructure**
- **Feature extraction:** `feasibility.finetune._extract_features_batch()` handles both frozen and partial unfreezing
- **Backbone loading:** `feasibility.model.load_backbone()` supports all 6 TSFM backbones with auto-detection
- **Normalization ablation ready:** Layer-normalization placement data exist in Appendix Table 5 (`tab:revin_placement`)

#### 6. **Evaluation & Reporting**
- **Metrics computed:** MSE, MAE, denormalized MSE/MAE, routing entropy, per-sample gate variance, cross-sample routing variance
- **Routing diagnostics:** `get_routing_stats()`, `get_gate_stats()` (mean per-expert gates), `get_alpha_values()` (for dual-stream)
- **Early stopping:** Val-based checkpoint restoration already in place for residual-ia

---

### (C) PRIORITY RANKING FOR NEXT EXPERIMENTS

1. **HIGH PRIORITY (Directly address weaknesses):**
   - Run residual-ia dose-response (partial normalization) on all 6 backbones to show non-triviality of fix
   - Complete self-routed results comparison vs RR-MoA (missing quantitative evidence in paper)
   - Run residual-ia on MOMENT-large and Moirai-MoE systematically (shows scalability)

2. **MEDIUM PRIORITY (Strengthen theory):**
   - Co-adaptation feedback quantification by layer (unfreeze=last-k, track per-layer entropy)
   - PatchTST adapter integration test (novel backbone architecture)
   - Per-expert co-adaptation measurement (which experts drift most?)

3. **LOWER PRIORITY (Incremental):**
   - Heterogeneous raw-branch depths per expert (adds complexity)
   - Router regularization to block (M,Σ) leakage learning

---

I now have a thorough understanding of the codebase, paper state, existing experiments, and weaknesses. Let me formulate the experiment plan.

---

## Prioritized Experiment Plan for NeurIPS 2026 Submission

Below is a plan for 4 experiments, ranked by expected reviewer impact per GPU-hour invested. I have designed each to address a specific weakness a skeptical reviewer would target, using existing infrastructure wherever possible.

---

### Experiment 1: Complete and Quantify the Self-Routed MoA (SR-MoA) Evidence

**What it tests / which weakness it addresses.**
This addresses weakness #1 ("obvious fix"). The paper currently claims SR-MoA "outperforms RR-MoA by 13-42% across all 6 datasets" and "generalizes across four backbones" (line 338 of main.tex), backed by 174 runs total and Table `tab:self_routed`. However, the self_routed_moa results directory is empty locally, suggesting those 174 results exist only on the RACE VM and haven't been rsynced back. More critically, the SR-MoA data is the paper's single strongest rebuttal to "obvious fix" -- it shows that routing on raw input is so robust that you can eliminate the external router entirely and let each expert self-gate. The raw vs. hidden-state control is the clearest version of the diagnosis because it isolates the routing input variable with zero architectural confound (same sigmoid gate, same experts, same training -- only the input changes).

A skeptical reviewer who sees Table `tab:self_routed` in appendix but no verify.py checks for it, and only 3 datasets in the routing input control section (ETTh1, ETTm1, Weather), would note: (a) incomplete dataset coverage (only 3 of 6 datasets have raw-vs-hidden), (b) the "174 runs" are not independently verifiable, and (c) the freeze-level ablation has only 3 datasets at 2 freeze levels. The srmoa_deep_race.sh orchestrator already exists with Batches A-D covering freeze sweep (18 runs), multi-horizon (27 runs), cross-backbone (27 runs), and full dataset grid (27+ runs).

**Concrete run count and estimated GPU time.**
- Rsync the existing 174 results from RACE VM: 0 GPU time, 5 minutes wall clock.
- Run `run_srmoa_deep_race.sh` Batch A (freeze sweep, 18 runs) + Batch C (cross-backbone, 27 runs): ~80 min total.
- Run `run_self_routed_race.sh` P2c (hidden-state control on remaining datasets ETTh2, ETTm2, Electricity, 9 runs): ~15 min.
- Total: ~54 new runs, ~1.5 hours GPU.

**What script to use.**
- Existing: `scripts/run_self_routed_moa.py` (the runner), `scripts/run_self_routed_race.sh` (P1-P3 orchestrator, 87 runs), `scripts/run_srmoa_deep_race.sh` (Batches A-D, 99 runs).
- Analysis: write a small `analyze_self_routed.py` modeled on `analyze_rescue_sweep.py`.
- Add verify.py checks for the raw-vs-hidden entropy and MSE numbers in `tab:self_routed`.

**Expected paper impact.**
- Strengthens Table `tab:self_routed` from 3-dataset routing control to 6-dataset routing control (all 6 show raw better than hidden), giving a clean "6/6 datasets" claim.
- Extends cross-backbone from 3 rows to the full SR-MoA cross-backbone grid (Moirai, Chronos, MOMENT-large).
- The freeze paradox replication becomes quantitative across all 6 datasets (currently 3).
- Crucially, this lets the paper say: "three independent experiments -- RR-MoA, SR-MoA, and AdaMix+raw-router -- all converge on the same diagnosis from orthogonal architectural angles." This turns the "obvious fix" criticism into a strength: the fix is obvious because the diagnosis is correct.

**Risk assessment.**
- Low risk. The script, orchestrator, and analysis pipeline all exist. The existing 174 results already show SR-MoA works; this just fills in gaps. Main risk is if the RACE VM is down or the self_routed_moa results got overwritten -- but the sr_moa/ directory has 18 results locally as fallback.

---

### Experiment 2: Hidden-State Collapse on All 6 Datasets for SR-MoA (Controlled Ablation Table)

**What it tests / which weakness it addresses.**
This directly addresses weakness #1 ("obvious fix") by turning the paper's qualitative claim into a quantitative controlled experiment table. Currently, the hidden-state control in `tab:self_routed` shows 3 datasets (ETTh1 +120%, ETTm1 +137%, Weather +130%). A reviewer will ask: "What about the other 3 datasets?" The run_self_routed_race.sh P2c section already generates this, but only for the 3 core datasets.

To make this a maximally tight controlled experiment, extend P2c to all 6 datasets with 5 seeds. This creates a proper 2x6 factorial table (raw vs hidden, 6 datasets) with p-values per cell. The same sigmoid gate architecture, same experts, same training -- the ONLY variable is what the gate reads. This is the cleanest experiment in the paper for isolating the normalization bottleneck.

**Concrete run count and estimated GPU time.**
- 6 datasets x 5 seeds x 2 conditions (raw + hidden) = 60 runs.
- Subtract existing: ~15 raw runs (from P1/P3a), ~9 hidden runs (from P2c).
- Net new: ~36 runs at ~60s each = ~36 min GPU.

**What script to use.**
Existing `scripts/run_self_routed_moa.py` with `--routing-input raw` vs `--routing-input hidden`. A small shell loop extending `run_self_routed_race.sh` P2c to the remaining 3 datasets with seeds {42,43,44,45,46}.

**Expected paper impact.**
- Enables the claim: "SR-MoA on raw input outperforms the same architecture on hidden states on 6/6 datasets (p<0.001, paired Wilcoxon), with MSE degradation ranging from +100% to +150%."
- This directly rebuts "the fix is obvious" by reframing it: "The fix follows necessarily from the diagnosis. Three orthogonal architectures all improve when switched from hidden to raw input, confirming the mechanism is normalization, not architecture."
- This is the paper's cleanest causal statement and should be in the main text, not just appendix.

**Risk assessment.**
- Extremely low risk. Same script, same datasets, same backbone. The hidden-state condition should consistently degrade because the mechanism is well-understood. The only possible surprise is if hidden-state SR-MoA somehow works on a dataset (which would itself be interesting and explainable via R(D)).

---

### Experiment 3: SR-MoA Cross-Backbone on Timer-XL (Negative Control)

**What it tests / which weakness it addresses.**
Addresses weakness #3 (single primary backbone). The paper's strongest cross-backbone evidence for the diagnosis is the Timer-XL "opposite entropy trajectory" (lines 330-331 in main.tex, Figure `fig:causal_contrast`). But this evidence is only for the AdaMix collapse direction. The paper does NOT test SR-MoA on Timer-XL. If SR-MoA on raw input does NOT outperform SR-MoA on hidden states for Timer-XL (since Timer-XL has no RevIN), this would be the prediction-confirming negative control: "On a backbone without RevIN, raw vs hidden makes no difference, exactly as Proposition 2 predicts."

This addresses both the "single backbone" weakness and strengthens the theory by showing the prediction works in both directions: raw routing helps where RevIN strips signal (MOMENT), and does NOT help where there is no RevIN (Timer-XL).

**Concrete run count and estimated GPU time.**
- Timer-XL, 3 datasets x 3 seeds x 2 conditions (raw + hidden) = 18 runs.
- At ~90s per run (Timer-XL): ~27 min GPU.

**What script to use.**
Existing `scripts/run_self_routed_moa.py` with `--backbone ThinkLab-HUST/timer-xl-t84m` (or whatever the Timer-XL identifier is). The `run_srmoa_deep_race.sh` Batch C already covers Moirai, Chronos, MOMENT-large but NOT Timer-XL. This would need a small addition to the shell script.

The Timer-XL backbone is already supported in the codebase -- evidenced by the 90 results in `results/adamix_timer/` and the causal contrast figure.

**Expected paper impact.**
- If raw = hidden on Timer-XL: "On Timer-XL (no RevIN), SR-MoA on raw input shows no advantage over hidden-state gating (p>0.05), confirming Proposition 2's prediction that normalization is the bottleneck."
- This creates a symmetric evidence structure: MOMENT (RevIN) shows raw >> hidden; Timer-XL (no RevIN) shows raw = hidden. This is the cleanest validation of the theory's predictive power.
- Moves the paper from "we found something" to "we have a predictive theory that says when this matters."

**Risk assessment.**
- Medium risk. Timer-XL may need some adaptation in `run_self_routed_moa.py` for backbone loading (the detect_backbone_type function needs to handle Timer-XL). Check if Timer-XL support already exists in the self-routed script. If not, it requires a small modification to add the backbone dispatch. However, `run_rr_moa.py` already supports Timer-XL (as evidenced by results/rr_moa/*bb-timer* files), so the code path likely exists.
- The bigger risk is if Timer-XL unexpectedly shows raw >> hidden, which would weaken the theory. But the existing Timer-XL AdaMix data (no collapse, healthy entropy) strongly suggests this won't happen.

---

### Experiment 4: Imputation Task Extension for SR-MoA + RR-MoA

**What it tests / which weakness it addresses.**
Addresses "just a forecasting paper" concern and strengthens cross-task generalization. The paper already mentions imputation results (ETTh1, ETTm1, Weather via `run_imputation.py`), but adding SR-MoA imputation creates a 2x2 matrix: (forecasting, imputation) x (RR-MoA, SR-MoA), all showing the same pattern. This is valuable because it shows the normalization bottleneck is task-agnostic, not specific to the forecasting objective.

**Concrete run count and estimated GPU time.**
- 3 datasets x 3 seeds x 2 methods (RR-MoA, SR-MoA) x 2 conditions (raw, hidden) = 36 runs.
- At ~60s per run: ~36 min GPU.
- But this may require modifying `run_self_routed_moa.py` to support imputation, which adds development time.

**What script to use.**
- Existing: `scripts/run_imputation.py` (for RR-MoA imputation baseline).
- New/modified: extend `run_self_routed_moa.py` to support imputation mode, or write a small wrapper.

**Expected paper impact.**
- "The normalization bottleneck holds across tasks: SR-MoA on raw input outperforms hidden-state gating on both forecasting (6/6 datasets) and imputation (3/3 datasets)."
- Moves the paper's scope from "forecasting adapter routing" to "general TSFM adapter routing."

**Risk assessment.**
- Medium-high risk. Requires modifying the SR-MoA script to support imputation, which may uncover edge cases. The imputation results already exist for RR-MoA, so the infrastructure is partially there, but the SR-MoA imputation combination is untested. Given the 12-day deadline, this has a higher chance of debugging-induced delays.

---

## Recommended Priority Ordering

Given 12 days to deadline, a single A10G, and the need for analysis + paper integration + verify.py checks per experiment:

1. **Experiment 1 + 2 combined (SR-MoA completion): ~2.5 hours GPU, 1-2 days total.** Highest impact-per-hour. The infrastructure is fully built. This turns a qualitative appendix claim into the paper's second-strongest quantitative result. Start with rsyncing existing results, then run the gap-filling sweeps.

2. **Experiment 3 (Timer-XL negative control for SR-MoA): ~30 min GPU, 0.5-1 day total.** The predictive-negative-control aspect ("we predicted it wouldn't help, and it didn't") is extremely compelling to NeurIPS reviewers who care about scientific rigor over method novelty. Small GPU cost, bounded scope.

3. **Experiment 4 (Imputation extension): ~1 day total if modification is straightforward.** Lower priority because the paper already mentions imputation results for RR-MoA. Only pursue if Experiments 1-3 complete with time to spare.

The combined GPU cost of Experiments 1-3 is approximately 3.5 hours. Even with 4x overhead for debugging, rsync delays, and analysis, this fits comfortably in 3-4 days of a 12-day window, leaving 8+ days for paper integration, verify.py extension, and final writing.

**What NOT to do:**
- Do not run more MOMENT-small primary backbone experiments. 54/54 wins with 1,302 rr_moa results is already saturated. Diminishing returns.
- Do not extend the rescue sweep beyond 720 runs. 11 mechanisms x 720 runs is already overwhelming evidence.
- Do not add new architectures or new backbones beyond Timer-XL. Breadth of the existing 5 backbones is sufficient; depth of the SR-MoA controlled ablation is what's missing.
- Do not chase incremental DLinear gap improvements. 108/123 is strong; trying to push to 115/123 would require large GPU budgets with small reviewer benefit.

### Critical Files for Implementation
- `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/scripts/run_self_routed_moa.py` -- the SR-MoA runner, needs minor extension for Timer-XL backbone support
- `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/scripts/run_srmoa_deep_race.sh` -- existing orchestrator for Batches A-D; needs Timer-XL addition and hidden-state control extension
- `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/scripts/run_self_routed_race.sh` -- existing orchestrator for P1-P3; P2c section needs extension to all 6 datasets
- `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/evidence_vm/verify.py` -- must add SR-MoA table checks (raw vs hidden MSE, entropy, cross-backbone)
- `/Users/hungpq2412/neuralips26/main.tex` -- the paper itself, where SR-MoA results need promotion from thin appendix table to quantitative controlled-experiment evidence in the main text

1	"""Self-Routed Mixture of Adapters (SR-MoA).
2	
3	Eliminates the external router entirely. Each expert has an internal gate
4	that determines its activation from raw input — experts route themselves.
5	
6	Inspired by ERMoE (arXiv:2511.10971), AoE (arXiv:2501.13074, ICML 2025),
7	and Routing-Free MoE (arXiv:2604.00801). Extends the RR-MoA diagnosis:
8	if normalization kills external routers, the principled fix is to eliminate
9	the router and let each expert self-determine its activation on raw input.
10	
11	Three self-routing modes:
12	  - gated:      per-expert sigmoid gate on raw input (primary)
13	  - eigenbasis: cosine similarity to learned orthogonal basis vectors
14	  - hybrid:     sigmoid of cosine similarity (combines both)
15	
16	Usage:
17	    python scripts/run_self_routed_moa.py --dataset ETTh1 --routing-mode gated
18	    python scripts/run_self_routed_moa.py --dataset ETTh1 --routing-mode eigenbasis
19	"""
20	
21	import argparse
22	import json
23	import math
24	import os
25	import sys
26	import time
27	
28	sys.path.insert(0, os.path.dirname(os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(__file__))))
29	
30	import numpy as np
31	import torch
32	import torch.nn as nn
33	import torch.nn.functional as F
34	from torch.utils.data import DataLoader, TensorDataset
35	
36	from feasibility.model import (
37	    load_backbone, _get_encoder_blocks, _get_hidden_dim,
38	    _disable_gradient_checkpointing,
39	)
40	from feasibility.finetune import _extract_features_batch
41	from scripts.run_standard_evolution import (
42	    load_standard_data, _detect_backbone_type, compute_denorm_mse,
43	)
44	
45	
46	# --- Expert adapter heads (same as RR-MoA for fair comparison) ---
47	
48	class MeanPoolHead(nn.Module):
49	    def __init__(self, d_model, output_dim, hidden=64):
50	        super().__init__()
51	        self.net = nn.Sequential(nn.Linear(d_model, hidden), nn.GELU(), nn.Linear(hidden, output_dim))
52	    def forward(self, h):
53	        return self.net(h.mean(dim=1))
54	
55	class LastTokenHead(nn.Module):
56	    def __init__(self, d_model, output_dim, hidden=64):
57	        super().__init__()
58	        self.net = nn.Sequential(nn.Linear(d_model, hidden), nn.GELU(), nn.Linear(hidden, output_dim))
59	    def forward(self, h):
60	        return self.net(h[:, -1, :])
61	
62	class MaxPoolHead(nn.Module):
63	    def __init__(self, d_model, output_dim, hidden=64):
64	        super().__init__()
65	        self.net = nn.Sequential(nn.Linear(d_model, hidden), nn.GELU(), nn.Linear(hidden, output_dim))
66	    def forward(self, h):
67	        return self.net(h.max(dim=1).values)
68	
69	class AttentionPoolHead(nn.Module):
70	    def __init__(self, d_model, output_dim, hidden=64):
71	        super().__init__()
72	        self.attn = nn.Linear(d_model, 1)
73	        self.net = nn.Sequential(nn.Linear(d_model, hidden), nn.GELU(), nn.Linear(hidden, output_dim))
74	    def forward(self, h):
75	        w = torch.softmax(self.attn(h), dim=1)
76	        return self.net((h * w).sum(dim=1))
77	
78	class Conv1dPoolHead(nn.Module):
79	    def __init__(self, d_model, output_dim, hidden=64):
80	        super().__init__()
```

84:\textbf{MoE routing collapse and rescue.} Routing collapse---where a few experts capture all traffic---is a well-known failure mode in MoE training. Existing solutions attack it from the \emph{optimization side}: load-balancing losses~\citep{fedus2022switch}, z-loss~\citep{zoph2022stmoe}, Expert-Choice routing~\citep{zhou2022expertchoice}, and ReLU gating~\citep{zhang2025remoe}. AdaMix~\citep{wang2022adamix} brings MoE to adapter fine-tuning in NLP but still routes on hidden states. All of these enforce diversity through training objectives without asking whether the router's \emph{input} contains discriminative information in the first place. We sweep eleven such mechanisms on MOMENT$+$RevIN and show \textbf{none recover MSE to within $2.7{\times}$ of RR-MoA} (Table~\ref{tab:rescue}). Our contribution is complementary: we show that when normalization structurally removes routing information from hidden states, no optimization trick can compensate. Concurrently, recent work in NLP and vision has moved toward eliminating external routers entirely: AoE~\citep{lv2025aoe} lets experts self-select via activation norms, ERMoE~\citep{cheng2025ermoe} routes via cosine similarity to learned eigenbases (achieving 0\% collapse), and Routing-Free MoE~\citep{liu2026routingfree} encapsulates gating within each expert. We validate this self-routing principle in our setting (\S\ref{sec:main_results}): self-gated experts on raw input match RR-MoA, while the same architecture on hidden states collapses---confirming the bottleneck is normalization, not router design.
276:{\looseness=-1 \textbf{The Frozen Paradox.} Frozen RR-MoA beats \emph{full fine-tuning} (all 8 blocks unfrozen, best of 5 heads $\times$ 2 LRs) by $12$--$79\%$ on every dataset (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}, Figure~\ref{fig:frozen_paradox}). The explanation is that unfreezing the backbone triggers the co-adaptation loop (Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen}): the dominant expert reshapes the shared representation, collapsing routing. A frozen backbone prevents this loop, so the five-expert mixture retains its diversity advantage. Within RR-MoA, frozen is best on 4/6 datasets; light unfreezing wins by up to $13\%$ on the other two. SR-MoA independently replicates this pattern (frozen best on 2/3 datasets; Table~\ref{tab:self_routed}). Extended fine-tuning (50 epochs, cosine schedule) closes the gap by only ${\sim}4\%$ (Appendix~\ref{app:extended_ft}).}
338:\textbf{Self-routing and architecture-agnostic validation.} To confirm the bottleneck is in the routing \emph{input} rather than the router \emph{architecture}, we test two additional controls. First, Self-Routed MoA (SR-MoA)~\citep{lv2025aoe,cheng2025ermoe,liu2026routingfree}: each expert contains its own sigmoid gate---no external router. On raw input, SR-MoA \emph{outperforms} RR-MoA by $13$--$42\%$ across all 6 datasets (ETTh1: $0.468$; ETTm1: $0.389$; Weather: $0.221$; 174 runs total), generalizes across four backbones, and independently replicates the Frozen Paradox (frozen beats last-4 on 2/3 datasets; Table~\ref{tab:self_routed}). The same architecture gating on hidden states collapses ($2.0$--$2.3\times$ worse, entropy ${\to}\log K$). Second, replacing AdaMix's hidden-state router with a raw-input router recovers entropy to $1.41$--$1.57$ and MSE by $41$--$88\%$ on all 12 cells (Appendix~\ref{app:adamix_details}). Both confirm the diagnosis: the bottleneck is normalization, not router design.
1206:\caption{\textbf{Self-Routed MoA (SR-MoA): routing input control} (dense, 3 seeds except $^{\dagger}$5+ seeds). Each expert has its own sigmoid gate---no external router. \textbf{Top}: gating on raw input vs hidden states (frozen). \textbf{Middle}: freeze-level ablation confirms Frozen Paradox. \textbf{Bottom}: cross-backbone generalization (H=96, frozen). 174 runs total.}
1207:\label{tab:self_routed}

1244	\textbf{What the router learns.} Per-sample routing decisions correlate with amplitude and volatility---the very statistics RevIN strips (Figure~\ref{fig:routing_viz}). On ETTh1, Expert~0 (mean-pool) serves as the generalist ($53\%$), while Expert~2 (max-pool) handles low-amplitude windows and Expert~3 (attention-pool) handles high-amplitude windows. The router reserves specialized processing for windows at the distribution tails, empirically validating the conditional independence assumption $E \perp\!\!\!\perp S \mid (M,\Sigma)$ of Part~(iii) of Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}.
1245	
1246	\begin{figure}[h]
1247	\centering
1248	\includegraphics[width=0.95\columnwidth]{figures/routing_viz.pdf}
1249	\caption{\textbf{Per-sample expert assignment vs.\ input statistics} (ETTh1, seed 42, $N{=}5{,}000$ test windows). Left: amplitude vs.\ volatility; right: mean level vs.\ amplitude. Colors indicate dominant expert (top-1 routing weight). Expert selection clusters by amplitude and volatility---the very statistics RevIN strips---empirically validating the conditional independence assumption $(M,\Sigma) \perp\!\!\!\perp S$ from Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}(iii).}
1250	\label{fig:routing_viz}
1251	\end{figure}
1252	
1253	\textbf{Regime-specific adaptation.} Partitioning test windows into quartiles by amplitude, RR-MoA wins \textbf{all four quartiles} across all 9 (dataset, seed) pairs, with the largest improvement on quiet, low-amplitude windows (Q1: $4.6{\times}$ better, $0.24$ vs $1.09$ on ETTh1) and the smallest on volatile, high-amplitude windows (Q4: $1.3{\times}$, $1.54$ vs $1.97$).
1254	
1255	\textbf{Sufficient statistic router (SSR).} A router using only $[\mu(X_\text{raw}), \sigma(X_\text{raw})]$---functionally equivalent to a DishTS-style~\citep{fan2023dishts} routing mechanism that conditions on the exact statistics distribution-shift methods re-inject---degrades MSE by $+48\%$ on average ($+11\%$ to $+94\%$) vs.\ the Conv1d router across all 8 forecasting datasets. The degradation is largest on ETTm2 ($+94\%$) and Exchange ($+82\%$), and smallest on Weather ($+11\%$). While Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp} correctly identifies the \emph{direction} of the information loss, effective routing requires temporal shape patterns beyond location-scale statistics.
1256	
1257	\textbf{Temporal-shuffle ablation.} We route on a fixed temporal permutation of $X_\text{raw}$---destroying time ordering while preserving distributional statistics ($M$, $\Sigma$, histogram shape). Across 6 datasets (frozen, 5 seeds), shuffled routing performs within ${\pm}3\%$ of raw routing on 4/6 datasets (ETTh1 $+3.2\%$, ETTh2 $+1.2\%$, ETTm1 $-1.2\%$, Electricity $+0.7\%$), with Weather improving $-8.5\%$ and ETTm2 improving $-16.0\%$. Entropy remains high ($1.43$--$1.58$). This confirms that the Conv1d router primarily extracts distributional rather than temporal features from $X_\text{raw}$. Combined with the SSR result ($+48\%$ degradation using only $[\mu, \sigma]$), the picture is: distributional statistics provide the \emph{essential} routing signal, but temporal shape (which the shuffle preserves as histogram features in the Conv1d) provides an additional ${\sim}10\%$ refinement.
1258	
1259	\textbf{Router-Detached Gradient Flow (RDGF).} A variant that unfreezes the backbone but replaces routing-weighted gradients with uniform-average gradients ($\nabla_H \mathcal{L}_\text{backbone} = \frac{1}{K}\sum_k \nabla_H \mathcal{L}_k$) degrades MSE by $+35\%$ to $+100\%$ across three datasets. This confirms the Frozen Paradox is structural.
1260	
1261	\textbf{Spectral-temporal router (FFT-Router).} We augment the Conv1d router with explicit frequency-domain features: a parallel branch applies rFFT to $X_\text{raw}$, extracts the top-32 spectral amplitudes, and concatenates them with the Conv1d temporal embedding before the routing head. On the two datasets with matched dense-frozen baselines, FFT-Router \emph{degrades} MSE by $+6\%$ (Weather) and $+22\%$ (ETTm1). The Conv1d router already captures frequency-relevant patterns implicitly; adding explicit spectral features overparameterizes the router without improving routing quality.
1262	
1263	\textbf{Statistic-aware information bottleneck (SAIB).} An auxiliary loss forces the router's penultimate representation to predict the window-level $[\mu, \sigma]$---directly operationalizing Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}. With SAIB coefficients $\lambda \in \{0.1, 1.0\}$, MSE is unchanged on ETTm1 ($0.395$ vs $0.397$ baseline) and slightly degraded on Weather ($0.250$ vs $0.236$). Routing entropy increases to $1.55$ (near-maximum $\log 5 = 1.61$), indicating the auxiliary loss disperses routing weights without improving forecast accuracy. This confirms the Conv1d router already encodes $\mu, \sigma$ without explicit supervision.
1264	
1265	\textbf{Instance-adaptive gating (IA-Gating).} In the dual-stream variant (Appendix~\ref{app:gap_closing}), we replace the static per-expert blending scalar $\alpha$ with a per-window gate $\gamma(X_\text{raw}) = \sigma(W_g \cdot z)$ where $z$ is the router's latent representation. Across 6 datasets (frozen, 3 seeds), IA-Gating wins on 4/6 (ETTh2 $-8.0\%$, ETTm2 $-6.4\%$, ETTm1 $-1.9\%$, Electricity $-0.4\%$) and loses on 2/6 (Weather $+6.1\%$, ETTh1 $+0.3\%$). The learned gates cluster tightly around $\gamma \approx 0.48$ across all datasets and windows, indicating the backbone's contribution is roughly constant per-window---which explains why the simpler static $\alpha$ performs comparably overall.
1266

476	        suffixes.append("bb-moirai")
477	    elif "chronos" in bb_lower:
478	        suffixes.append("bb-chronos")
479	    elif args.backbone != "AutonLab/MOMENT-1-small":
480	        suffixes.append("bb-" + args.backbone.split("/")[-1].lower())
481	
482	    suffix = "_".join(suffixes)
483	    path = "%s/%s_H%d_K%d_%s_%d_%s.json" % (
484	        args.results_dir, args.dataset, args.horizon, args.K,
485	        args.unfreeze, args.seed, suffix)
486	
487	    scaler_info = None
488	    if scaler is not None:
489	        scaler_info = {
490	            "scale_": [float(x) for x in scaler.scale_],
491	            "mean_": [float(x) for x in scaler.mean_],
492	            "n_features_in_": int(getattr(scaler, "n_features_in_", len(scaler.scale_))),
493	            "mean_scale_sq": float(np.mean(scaler.scale_ ** 2)),
494	        }
495	
496	    save_data = {
497	        "dataset": args.dataset, "horizon": args.horizon, "seed": args.seed,
498	        "K": args.K, "unfreeze": args.unfreeze,
499	        "routing_mode": args.routing_mode,
500	        "routing_input": args.routing_input,
501	        "gate_hidden": args.gate_hidden if args.routing_mode == "gated" else None,
502	        "basis_dim": args.basis_dim if args.routing_mode != "gated" else None,
503	        "temperature": args.temperature if args.routing_mode != "gated" else None,
504	        "gate_init_bias": args.gate_init_bias,
505	        "backbone_trainable_params": backbone_trainable,
506	        "sr_moa": result,
507	        "elapsed": elapsed,
508	        "scaler": scaler_info,
509	    }

1. **E1: Raw-vs-hidden control on ALL 6 datasets** (21 runs) — upgrades the cleanest causal table from 3/6 to 6/6 datasets. Directly rebuts "obvious fix" by showing three orthogonal architectures converge.

2. **E2: Timer-XL negative control** (30 runs) — the theory predicts raw ≈ hidden on Timer-XL (no RevIN). If confirmed, this is a falsifiable prediction that holds in both directions. Strongest possible theory validation.

3. **E3: Freeze ablation all 6 datasets** (18 runs) — replicates Frozen Paradox through SR-MoA independently, upgrading from 3 to 6 datasets.

4. **E4: Cross-backbone expansion** (18 runs) — adds Moirai-MoE + Timer-XL to the SR-MoA grid, matching RR-MoA's 6-backbone coverage.

All use the existing `run_self_routed_moa.py` — no new training code needed. Total wall-clock ~1.5 hours with 2 workers, leaving ample time for analysis + paper integration.
