```
54:Mixture-of-expert adapters with learned routers are a natural way to adapt frozen time series foundation models (TSFMs)---yet they \textbf{catastrophically fail} on TSFMs with instance normalization: routing entropy collapses to $0.000{\pm}0.000$ because the normalization strips the per-window statistics the router needs. We isolate this \textbf{normalization-induced routing collapse} through seven converging causal controls---most strikingly, opposite entropy trajectories on identical data (Timer-XL rises $1.49{\to}1.60$ while MOMENT collapses $0.60{\to}0.00$)---while all eleven standard MoE-rescue mechanisms fail ($2.7{\times}$ worse; 720 runs). A mutual-information decomposition formalizes the mechanism: normalization destroys routing MI by exactly the information in the stripped statistics. The resulting signal-ratio predictor identifies which datasets benefit ($\rho{=}{-}0.88$, $p{<}0.002$, $n{=}9$), including where it does \emph{not} help (Traffic, $R{=}0.14$). Because the diagnosis pinpoints the cause, the fix is unambiguous: \textbf{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)} routes on raw, pre-normalization input. RR-MoA achieves \textbf{54/54 wins} ($p{<}0.001$), generalizes across six backbones (two negative controls confirm normalization-specificity), and produces a \textbf{Frozen Paradox} where frozen adapters beat full fine-tuning by $12$--$79\%$. A dual-stream extension, \textbf{Residual-IA\textsuperscript{+}}, achieves \textbf{parity-or-better with DLinear on $107/123$ cells} ($65$ significant wins vs $11$ losses), with $15/16$ losses at $H{\geq}192$ and $100\%$ on Timer-XL and Moirai-MoE.
68:\textbf{Our fix.} Because the diagnosis pinpoints normalization as the cause, the fix is unambiguous: \textbf{Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA)} routes on the raw, pre-normalization input $\mathbf{X}_\text{raw}$ while keeping the backbone strictly frozen. Only the router and five canonical expert heads are trainable. Eleven standard MoE-rescue mechanisms (load balancing, entropy regularization, z-loss, ReLU routing, expert-choice) all fail to recover performance ($2.7{\times}$ worse than RR-MoA across 720 runs; Table~\ref{tab:rescue}), confirming that the problem is in the router's \emph{input}, not its \emph{optimization}.
546:Top-2 sparse routing adds only $4.3$\,ms latency ($+10\%$) while retaining $63\%$ of the dense MSE gain. Scaling the expert pool from $K{=}3$ to $K{=}20$ changes MSE by less than $5\%$; notably, AdaMix at $K{=}15$ or $20$ maintains high entropy ($2.4$--$2.7$) yet its MSE remains $2$--$6{\times}$ worse than RR-MoA, confirming that the routing failure is informational rather than capacity-limited. RR-MoA's advantage extends beyond the primary horizon: it wins \textbf{24/24} configurations across $H{\in}\{96, 192, 336, 720\}$ and generalizes to imputation ($7/8$ datasets, $-44\%$ to $-77\%$) and classification ($86.5\%$ on BasicMotions; Appendix~\ref{app:topk},~\ref{app:imputation}).}
```

```
Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters for Time Series
Foundation Models
Anonymous Author 1
Anonymous Institution
author1@example.com

Anonymous Author 2
Anonymous Institution
author2@example.com

Anonymous Author 3
Anonymous Institution
author3@example.com

Abstract
Mixture-of-expert adapters with learned routers are a natural way to adapt frozen
time series foundation models (TSFMs)—yet they catastrophically fail on TSFMs
with instance normalization: routing entropy collapses to 0.000±0.000 because
the normalization strips the per-window statistics the router needs. We isolate
this normalization-induced routing collapse through seven converging causal
controls—most strikingly, opposite entropy trajectories on identical data (Timer-XL
rises 1.49→1.60 while MOMENT collapses 0.60→0.00)—while all eleven standard MoE-rescue mechanisms fail (2.7× worse; 720 runs). A mutual-information
decomposition formalizes the mechanism: normalization destroys routing MI by
exactly the information in the stripped statistics. The resulting signal-ratio predictor
identifies which datasets benefit (ρ=−0.88, p<0.002, n=9), including where it
does not help (Traffic, R=0.14). Because the diagnosis pinpoints the cause, the fix
is unambiguous: Raw-Routed Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA) routes on raw,
pre-normalization input. RR-MoA achieves 54/54 wins (p<0.001), generalizes
across six backbones (two negative controls confirm normalization-specificity),
and produces a Frozen Paradox where frozen adapters beat full fine-tuning by
12–79%. A dual-stream extension, Residual-IA+ , achieves parity-or-better with
DLinear on 107/123 cells (65 significant wins vs 11 losses), with 15/16 losses at
H≥192 and 100% on Timer-XL and Moirai-MoE.

1

Introduction

Time series foundation models (TSFMs)—MOMENT [6], TimesFM [7], Chronos [8], Timer-XL [10],
Moirai [11], and others [13–16]—provide powerful pretrained representations for temporal tasks.
Once a TSFM is pretrained, a practical question determines whether it is useful in production: how
should the adapter that connects it to a downstream task be designed?
The problem with one-size-fits-all adapters. Today, every public TSFM benchmark adapts the
backbone with a single static head that pools hidden states and projects them to the forecast horizon [6,
7, 17, 20]. But real time series are non-stationary—ETTh1 mixes quiet baseline segments with sharp
seasonal excursions—and a single adapter topology cannot be optimal for every input window. A
natural remedy is a mixture of expert adapters: a pool of topologically distinct heads with a learned
router that assigns each window to the most suitable expert.
Why keep the backbone frozen? Frozen-backbone adaptation enables deployment regimes that
per-dataset models like DLinear [22] cannot serve: multi-tenant cloud serving with hot-swappable
adapters, on-device deployment with over-the-air adapter updates, and cross-task reuse (Appendices S, S.1). As TSFMs scale toward billion-parameter regimes [15], frozen-backbone PEFT
becomes the primary per-dataset adaptation path.
The collapse problem. This natural mixture-of-experts approach catastrophically fails on TSFMs
that use instance normalization. When the standard MoE recipe [23, 24]—routing on hidden states
Preprint.

with load balancing—is applied to MOMENT with RevIN, routing entropy drops to 0.000 ± 0.000 in
7/12 unfrozen configurations (Table 2). The root cause is normalization-induced co-adaptation: the
dominant expert receives larger gradients, which reshape the backbone to serve it even more, creating
a feedback loop that collapses within ∼50 training steps (Figure 2). Even with a frozen backbone,
hidden-state routing still underperforms because RevIN [26] strips the per-window mean and variance
that the router needs to distinguish different input regimes. This is normalization-specific: Moirai [11],
which uses LayerNorm instead of RevIN, does not collapse (§4.2).
Our fix. Because the diagnosis pinpoints normalization as the cause, the fix is unambiguous: RawRouted Mixture of Adapters (RR-MoA) routes on the raw, pre-normalization input Xraw while
keeping the backbone strictly frozen. Only the router and five canonical expert heads are trainable.
Eleven standard MoE-rescue mechanisms (load balancing, entropy regularization, z-loss, ReLU
routing, expert-choice) all fail to recover performance (2.7× worse than RR-MoA across 720 runs;
Table I9), confirming that the problem is in the router’s input, not its optimization.
Our contributions are threefold:
(1) A general principle: normalization destroys routing information when the stripped statistics
carry the routing signal. A mutual-information decomposition (Proposition 2) proves that instance
normalization destroys routing MI by exactly the information in the stripped location-scale statistics.
The principle makes three falsifiable predictions: (a) a quantitative predictor R(D) identifies
which datasets benefit (ρ=−0.88, p<0.002, n=9) and which do not (Traffic, R=0.14; Figure 5);
(b) backbones without instance normalization should not collapse (confirmed: Timer-XL, Chronos;
§4.2); (c) the principle transfers across modalities (confirmed: ViT+InstanceNorm1d collapses;
ResNet+InstanceNorm2d does not; Appendix K.1). Seven converging causal controls isolate the
mechanism, the most striking being opposite entropy trajectories: MOMENT collapses (0.60→0.00)
while Timer-XL rises (1.49→1.60)—same code, same data, only the normalization differs.
(2) Fix and a predicted consequence. Because the principle pinpoints the cause, the fix is unambiguous: RR-MoA routes on raw pre-normalization input, achieving 54/54 wins (p<0.001; Table 3). The
principle also predicts a Frozen Paradox: unfreezing triggers co-adaptation collapse (Proposition 1),
so frozen adapters should beat full fine-tuning—confirmed at 12–79% across all six datasets. RRMoA generalizes across four RevIN backbones; two negative controls (Chronos, Timer-XL) confirm
normalization-specificity.
(3) Closing the DLinear gap—and explaining when it cannot close. Residual-IA+ adds a raw-input
prediction branch and achieves parity-or-better with DLinear on 107/123 cells across six backbones
(65 significant wins vs 11 losses; §4.2). The 16 remaining losses cluster at long horizons (15/16 at
H≥192), with the lone H=96 loss on the smallest dataset (ETTh1, +2.2%). Timer-XL (12/12) and
Moirai-MoE (15/15) achieve 100%.

2

Related Work

TSFM Adaptation. The standard approach to adapting a frozen TSFM is to attach a lightweight
module—LoRA [20], bottleneck adapters [21], or a linear probe [6]—and tune it on downstream data.
Recent work has expanded the design space: MSFT [29] proposes multi-scale finetuning, TRACE [30]
introduces importance-based LoRA selection with reconstructed heads, and AdaPTS [32] adapts
input representations rather than the backbone itself. Beyond LoRA [31] surveys PEFT alternatives
for Chronos; Prune-then-Finetune [33] applies structured pruning; in-context fine-tuning [34] adapts
at inference time. TEMPLATE [35] and GIFT-Eval [36] address model selection and benchmarking.
All of these search over finetuning strategies—learning rates, rank, which layers to tune—rather
than the adapter architecture itself. Our work addresses this gap: we show that the choice of adapter
topology should be per-sample, not per-dataset.
MoE in Foundation Models. Mixture-of-experts (MoE) routing [24] has been applied within TSFM
backbones to scale model capacity: Moirai-MoE [12] routes frequency bands across internal experts,
and Time-MoE [15] uses sparse activation to reach billions of parameters. These designs place
experts inside the backbone architecture. RR-MoA instead applies MoE at the adapter level on a
frozen backbone—an orthogonal design axis. We validate that the two axes stack: RR-MoA on top
of Moirai-MoE still yields −37% MSE (§4.2, Table R24).
2
```

It renders as "Table I9" — not `??`. This is the correct appendix numbering. But "Table I9" in the introduction does look odd to a reader who hasn't seen the appendix yet. The NeurIPS style uses letter-prefixed counters in appendix sections.

The issue is that the rescue table is in the appendix but referenced from the intro. This is normal and correct — "Table I9" tells the reader it's in appendix section I. No fix needed unless you want to move the table to the main body (no space for that).

```
diff --git a/main.tex b/main.tex
index 49fd074..247758a 100644
--- a/main.tex
+++ b/main.tex
@@ -59,7 +59,7 @@ Mixture-of-expert adapters with learned routers are a natural way to adapt froze
 
 Time series foundation models (TSFMs)---MOMENT~\citep{goswami2024moment}, TimesFM~\citep{das2024timesfm}, Chronos~\citep{ansari2024chronos}, Timer-XL~\citep{liu2025timerxl}, Moirai~\citep{woo2024moirai}, and others~\citep{lee2024units, rasul2024lagllama, shi2024timemoe, ekambaram2024ttm}---provide powerful pretrained representations for temporal tasks. Once a TSFM is pretrained, a practical question determines whether it is useful in production: \textit{how should the adapter that connects it to a downstream task be designed?}
 
-\textbf{The problem with one-size-fits-all adapters.} Today, every public TSFM benchmark adapts the backbone with a single static head: pool hidden states, project to the forecast horizon~\citep{goswami2024moment, das2024timesfm, nie2023patchtst, hu2022lora}. But real time series are non-stationary---ETTh1 mixes quiet baseline segments with sharp seasonal excursions---and a single adapter topology cannot be optimal for every input window. A natural remedy is a \emph{mixture of expert adapters}: a pool of topologically distinct heads with a learned router that assigns each window to the most suitable expert.
+\textbf{The problem with one-size-fits-all adapters.} Today, every public TSFM benchmark adapts the backbone with a single static head that pools hidden states and projects them to the forecast horizon~\citep{goswami2024moment, das2024timesfm, nie2023patchtst, hu2022lora}. But real time series are non-stationary---ETTh1 mixes quiet baseline segments with sharp seasonal excursions---and a single adapter topology cannot be optimal for every input window. A natural remedy is a \emph{mixture of expert adapters}: a pool of topologically distinct heads with a learned router that assigns each window to the most suitable expert.
 
 \textbf{Why keep the backbone frozen?} Frozen-backbone adaptation enables deployment regimes that per-dataset models like DLinear~\citep{zeng2023dlinear} cannot serve: multi-tenant cloud serving with hot-swappable adapters, on-device deployment with over-the-air adapter updates, and cross-task reuse (Appendices~\ref{app:deployment},~\ref{app:benchmark}). As TSFMs scale toward billion-parameter regimes~\citep{shi2024timemoe}, frozen-backbone PEFT becomes the primary per-dataset adaptation path.
 
@@ -69,7 +69,7 @@ Time series foundation models (TSFMs)---MOMENT~\citep{goswami2024moment}, TimesF
 
 Our contributions are threefold:
 
-\textbf{(1)~A general principle: normalization destroys routing information when the stripped statistics carry the routing signal.} A mutual-information decomposition (Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}) proves that instance normalization destroys routing MI by exactly the information in the stripped location-scale statistics. The principle makes three \textbf{falsifiable predictions}: (a)~a quantitative predictor $R(\mathcal{D})$ identifies which datasets benefit ($\rho{=}{-}0.88$, $p{<}0.002$, $n{=}9$) and which do not (Traffic, $R{=}0.14$; Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}); (b)~backbones without instance normalization should not collapse (confirmed: Timer-XL, Chronos; \S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}); (c)~the principle transfers across modalities (confirmed: ViT$+$InstanceNorm1d collapses; ResNet$+$InstanceNorm2d does not; Appendix~\ref{app:vision_moe}). Seven converging causal controls isolate the mechanism, the most striking being opposite entropy trajectories: MOMENT collapses ($0.60{\to}0.00$) while Timer-XL \emph{rises} ($1.49{\to}1.60$)---same code, same data, only the normalization differs.
+\textbf{(1)~A general principle: normalization destroys routing information when the stripped statistics carry the routing signal.} A mutual-information decomposition (Proposition~\ref{thm:mi_decomp}) proves that instance normalization destroys routing MI by exactly the information in the stripped location-scale statistics. The principle makes three \textbf{testable predictions}: (a)~a quantitative predictor $R(\mathcal{D})$ identifies which datasets benefit ($\rho{=}{-}0.88$, $p{<}0.002$, $n{=}9$) and which do not (Traffic, $R{=}0.14$; Figure~\ref{fig:signal_ratio}); (b)~backbones without instance normalization should not collapse (confirmed: Timer-XL, Chronos; \S\ref{sec:cross_backbone}); (c)~the principle transfers across modalities (confirmed: ViT$+$InstanceNorm1d collapses; ResNet$+$InstanceNorm2d does not; Appendix~\ref{app:vision_moe}). Seven converging causal controls isolate the mechanism, the most striking being opposite entropy trajectories: MOMENT collapses ($0.60{\to}0.00$) while Timer-XL \emph{rises} ($1.49{\to}1.60$)---same code, same data, only the normalization differs.
 
 \textbf{(2)~Fix and a predicted consequence.} Because the principle pinpoints the cause, the fix is unambiguous: RR-MoA routes on raw pre-normalization input, achieving \textbf{54/54 wins} ($p{<}0.001$; Table~\ref{tab:baselines}). The principle also predicts a \textbf{Frozen Paradox}: unfreezing triggers co-adaptation collapse (Proposition~\ref{prop:frozen}), so frozen adapters should beat full fine-tuning---confirmed at $12$--$79\%$ across all six datasets. RR-MoA generalizes across four RevIN backbones; two negative controls (Chronos, Timer-XL) confirm normalization-specificity.
```
